Question 1: question_1

❓ Question

How do I request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on FASRC Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/run.sbatch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on the FASRC cluster, add the `--gpus` flag to your script: ```bash #SBATCH --gpus=1 ``` You can also request CPUs and GPUs independently. For example, to request 1 CPU and 2 GPUs from the `gpu` partition: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH -n 1 #SBATCH --gpus=2 ``` When you submit a GPU job, SLURM automatically assigns GPUs and restricts your job to them. Inside your code, you reference these GPUs using zero-based indexing (e.g., `gpu:0`, `gpu:1`) [GPU Computing on the FASRC cluster](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster/). **Note:** Remember to load the CUDA module in your script or interactive session to access the toolkit and runtime libraries: ```bash module load cuda/<version> ```
✅ Expected Answer
Add #SBATCH --gpus=1 to your submission script and SLURM will give the job access to one GPU.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

For example, to request 1 CPU and 2 GPUs from the `gpu` partition: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH -n 1 #SBATCH --gpus=2 ``` When you submit a GPU job, SLURM automatically assigns GPUs and restricts your job to them. Inside your code, you reference these GPUs using zero-based indexing (e.g., `gpu:0`, `gpu:1`) [GPU Computing on the FASRC cluster](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster/). **Note:** Remember to load the CUDA module in your script or interactive session to access the toolkit and runtime libraries: ```bash module load cuda/<version> ```

Question 3: question_3

❓ Question

How much memory does a SLURM job get on Cannon if I don't specify a memory request?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
If you don't specify a memory request (`--mem`) in your SLURM job script, the job will be allocated only **100 MB per node** by default [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). If your job needs more memory than that, it will fail with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` error. To avoid this, always explicitly request the memory your job needs using the `--mem` flag in your job script, like this: ```bash #SBATCH --mem=8G ``` This ensures your job gets the memory it requires and runs successfully.
✅ Expected Answer
If you omit the memory request you are granted 100 MB by default, which is usually too little and will likely cause the job to be killed for going over.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs
💬 Agent Messages (2)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: How much memory does a SLURM job get on Cannon if I don't specify a memory request?
🤖 Assistant Message #2
If you don't specify a memory request (`--mem`) in your SLURM job script, the job will be allocated only **100 MB per node** by default [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). If your job needs more memory than that, it will fail with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` error. To avoid this, always explicitly request the memory your job needs using the `--mem` flag in your job script, like this: ```bash #SBATCH --mem=8G ``` This ensures your job gets the memory it requires and runs successfully.

Question 4: question_4

❓ Question

What is the storage quota for a FASRC user home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Your home directory has a **100 GB hard quota** and a **95 GB soft quota** that triggers notifications when you approach the limit [Home directory full – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/). You can check your current usage and quota with the `quota` command: ```bash quota $HOME ``` If you're over quota, you'll need to remove files to get back under the limit [Checking quota and usage – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage/).
✅ Expected Answer
Each user gets a 100 GB home directory, enforced as a hard limit.

Question 5: question_5

❓ Question

How much storage does a FASRC lab group directory get, and how many files?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-ownership-and-access-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-deletion-recommendations

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Each FASRC lab group receives a **4 TiB** lab directory (as of 2025) located at `/n/holylabs/<lab_name>`. This allocation has a **hard limit of 1 million files** in addition to the 4 TiB size cap [Home and Lab directories](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). Key details: - **Size:** 4 TiB (hard limit) - **File count:** 1 million files (hard limit) - **Path:** `/n/holylabs/<lab_name>` - **Backup:** Highly redundant, but no snapshots/backups - **Retention:** Duration of the lab group - **Performance:** Moderate — not appropriate for I/O-intensive or large numbers of jobs If your lab needs more storage, FASRC offers additional paid options like Lab Storage, Compute Storage, and Long-term (Tape) Storage. See the [Data Storage page](https://www.rc.fas.harvard.edu/data-storage/) for details.
✅ Expected Answer
Each lab gets a 4 TiB lab directory as a hard limit, with a 1 million file limit.

Lab directories reside under /n/holylabs.

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/access-from-abroad, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cant-login-cluster-access

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
SSH to **`login.rc.fas.harvard.edu`** using your FASRC username. For example: ```bash ssh your_username@login.rc.fas.harvard.edu ``` You'll be prompted for your password and two-factor authentication code. See the [Command line access with Terminal (login nodes)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access/) and [About Usernames](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames/) pages for more details.

❓ Question

What command shows my lab's fairshare information on FASRC?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/dual-lab-affiliations-on-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/additional-groups, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/job_exit_code.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sh5util.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/common-pitfalls, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/Large_Data_Processing_R/parallel_computation/R_parLapply_vdi.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/seas-compute-resources, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/lab-groups, slurm.schedmd.com/qos.html, slurm.schedmd.com, slurm.schedmd.com/documentation.html, slurm.schedmd.com/rest_api.html, slurm.schedmd.com/fair_tree.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/openapi_release_notes.html, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/classic_fair_share.html, slurm.schedmd.com/resource_limits.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, you can check your lab's fairshare score using the **`sshare`** command: ```bash sshare -u RCUSERNAME ``` Replace `RCUSERNAME` with your FASRC username. The effective score is shown in the last column of the output. As a rule of thumb, a score **≤ 0.5** indicates lower priority, while a score **> 0.5** indicates higher priority for job scheduling [Frequently Asked Questions (FAQ)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq/). You can also run `sshare -U` to check fairshare for your own account [Running Jobs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/).
✅ Expected Answer
Use sshare (e.g. sshare --account=<lab> -a) to see RawShares, NormShares, RawUsage, EffectvUsage, and the FairShare score for an account.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Args: fairshare
🤖 Assistant Message #7

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC home directory snapshots are **retained for 2 weeks** [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). The home directories have daily snapshots, and you can access them via the hidden `.snapshot` directory in your home. Hourly, daily, and weekly snapshots are available within that directory. Keep in mind that if you delete your entire home directory, the snapshots are also deleted and are not recoverable [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/).
✅ Expected Answer
Home directories have daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks, accessible via the .snapshot directory.

Question 11: question_11

❓ Question

My SLURM job on Cannon was killed for running out of memory. How do I find out how much memory it actually used, and how do I request more next time?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here’s how to check how much memory your job actually used and how to request more next time: ### 1. Find out how much memory your job used - **If the job completed successfully**, you can see its peak memory usage with: ```bash sacct -j <JOBID> --format=JobID,MaxRSS ``` or use the `jobstats` tool: ```bash jobstats <JOBID> ``` This will show the maximum resident set size (peak memory) used by the job. - **If the job was killed for `OUT_OF_MEMORY`**, the memory usage recorded by Slurm will be inaccurate. In that case, you’ll need to re-run the job with a successful completion to get a true peak memory measurement. ### 2. Request more memory for next time Add a `--mem` directive to your job script to request more memory per node: ```bash #SBATCH --mem 4G ``` This requests 4 GB of memory per node. Adjust the value based on what `jobstats` or `sacct` reports for your workload. It’s best to request slightly more than the peak usage you observe, since `--mem` sets a hard limit. ### Tips - Start with a generous request (job slots average ~4000 MB/core) and then refine using `jobstats` to see actual usage. - For multi-node jobs, `--mem` specifies the maximum memory used on **any one node**. Use `--ntasks-per-node` to control task distribution if needed. - You can also use `seff-account` to get summary memory usage across multiple jobs over time. For more details, see the FASRC documentation on [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/).
✅ Expected Answer
After the job ends, run sacct -j <jobid> --format=JobID,Elapsed,ReqMem,MaxRSS to compare the peak memory used (MaxRSS) against what was requested (ReqMem). In your next submit script raise the request with --mem=<N>G (whole job) or --mem-per-cpu=<N>G, setting it a bit above the observed MaxRSS so normal variation doesn't kill the job.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 12: question_12

❓ Question

Is the two-factor code for logging into FASRC the same as my Harvard Key code? If not, how do I set it up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**No, the FASRC two-factor code is not the same as your Harvard Key code.** They are separate tokens, but if you already have the Duo Mobile app installed for your Harvard Key, you can add your FASRC token to the same app. Here is how to set it up: 1. **Get the QR Code:** * From a computer (not your phone), visit [https://two-factor.rc.fas.harvard.edu](https://two-factor.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) and log in with your RC username and password. * Check your email for a link, click it, and a page with a QR code will appear. Leave this page open. 2. **Add to Duo Mobile:** * Open the Duo Mobile app on your phone and tap the **+** icon to add a new token. * Use your phone's camera to scan the QR code displayed on your computer screen. * Once scanned, Duo will add the token. You can now view your 6-digit FASRC OpenAuth code in the app. This code works for logging into the cluster, connecting to the RC VPN, and other services. For more details, see [Using Duo Mobile to display your RC two-factor code](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile/).
✅ Expected Answer
No. FASRC has its own two-factor system (OpenAuth) that is separate from Harvard Key. Set it up with a smartphone authenticator app (e.g. Google Authenticator or Duo) before accessing cluster resources; you use this OpenAuth token along with your FASRC username and password.

Question 13: question_13

❓ Question

How do I find which versions of a software package are available as modules on Cannon, and how should I load it so my workflow keeps working when defaults change?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To find available versions and load them reliably: ### 1. Find available versions Run one of these commands on Cannon: - **`module avail <package>`** — shows versions of a specific package that are loadable in your current environment (filtered by loaded compilers/MPI) [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/). - **`module --default avail <package>`** — same as above but shows the full list regardless of what you've already loaded. - **`module spider <package>`** — searches across all compilers/MPI variants and shows which versions are available under each combination [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/). ### 2. Load a specific version (recommended) Always specify the **full module name**, including version and build tag, to protect your workflow when defaults change: ```bash module load matlab/R2025b-fasrc01 module load gcc/15.2.0-fasrc01 openmpi/5.0.8-fasrc01 hdf5/1.14.6-fasrc01 ``` This is **strongly recommended** because the default version can change over time based on stability or common usage, which could silently break reproducibility [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/). ### 3. Verify what's loaded ```bash module list ``` ### Quick workflow tip If you frequently use the same set of modules, save and restore them: ```bash module save myworkflow # saves current set module restore myworkflow # restores it later ``` This way you can pin a known-good combination without hard-coding paths.
✅ Expected Answer
Search with module spider <name> to list all available modules matching the string regardless of your current environment. Then load with both the name and a specific version (module load <name>/<version>) — FASRC strongly recommends this so you are protected if the default version changes later.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Question 14: question_14

❓ Question

What's the recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment for a project on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/gReLU/grelu.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/jupyter-notebook-server-on-cluster

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment on Cannon is to use **Mamba** (a fast, drop-in replacement for Conda) inside a virtual environment. Direct use of `pip` outside of a virtual environment is discouraged on FASRC clusters. Here's the recommended workflow: 1. **Start an interactive job** (required for creating environments): ```bash salloc --partition test --nodes=1 --cpus-per-task=2 --mem=4GB --time=0-02:00:00 ``` 2. **Load the Python module** (which includes Mamba): ```bash module load python ``` 3. **Create an environment** (optionally with packages): ```bash mamba create -n my_env python=3.10 pip wheel ``` 4. **Activate the environment**: ```bash source activate my_env ``` 5. **Install additional packages** as needed: ```bash mamba install -y numpy pandas ``` **Key notes:** - By default, environments are created in `~/.conda/envs`. You can change this by setting `CONDA_ENVS_PATH` to a Lab shared directory if desired. - The FASRC `python` module is built with Miniforge3 and uses `conda-forge` as the default channel, since `repo.anaconda.com` is blocked on the cluster due to licensing restrictions. - Mamba is faster than Conda and fully compatible with Conda commands. For more details, see [Python Package Installation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation).
✅ Expected Answer
Load a module that provides mamba (python/3*, Miniforge3, or Mambaforge), then create an isolated environment, e.g. mamba create -n data_env python=3.9 pandas numpy, and activate it with source activate data_env. FASRC recommends using Miniforge with mamba so dependencies are isolated and you avoid conflicts rather than working in the base environment.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Question 15: question_15

❓ Question

How is my job's priority determined on Cannon, and how can I see the priority of my pending jobs?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
### How Job Priority Is Determined on Cannon Job priority on Cannon is calculated using two main components: 1. **FairShare Score:** This is the primary driver. Every lab (account) has a "Share" of the cluster resources. The FairShare score is calculated based on how much of that share the account has actually used. * A FairShare score of 1.0 means the account is using its fair share. * A score below 1.0 means the account has used less than its share and will receive a higher priority boost. * A score above 1.0 means the account has used more than its share and will receive a lower priority. * This score is multiplied by a weighting factor (10,000,000) to create the **FairShare Priority** component. [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/) 2. **Job Age:** Jobs that have been waiting in the pending queue longer accrue additional priority over time. This ensures that jobs don't wait indefinitely if higher-priority jobs keep arriving. [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/) The final job priority is a combination of these two factors, which determines the job's position in the pending queue. ### How to See the Priority of Your Pending Jobs You can view the priority of your jobs using the `squeue` command with the `--start` or `-p` flag to display the priority column. Run the following command: ```bash squeue -u $USER -o "%.10i %.9P %.10j %.8u %.2t %.10M %.6D %.10R %.6P %.10S %a" ``` Or more simply, to see the priority column explicitly: ```bash squeue -u $USER -o "jobid,priority,state" ``` The **priority** column will show the integer priority value for each of your pending jobs. Higher numbers indicate higher priority. [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/)
✅ Expected Answer
A job's priority combines two parts: your FairShare score multiplied by a weighting factor (10,000,000) to make it an integer, plus job age, which accrues while the job waits and caps at 3 days on Cannon. Inspect the breakdown for pending jobs with sprio, or use showq -o -p <partition> to see the pending queue ordered by priority.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 16: question_16

❓ Question

I need to move a very large dataset to the cluster. Should I use Globus, and can I transfer it straight into my home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/Large_Data_Processing_R/parallel_computation/R_parLapply_vdi.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here's the short answer: **Yes, use Globus for large transfers** — it's the recommended tool for copying very large datasets to/from FASRC systems [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). **No, you cannot transfer directly into your home directory via Globus.** Home directories are explicitly excluded from Globus access for security reasons (to prevent accidental sharing of authentication keys/passwords) [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). ### Recommended approach for large datasets: 1. **Transfer to your lab directory** (`/n/holylabs`) or **scratch** (`/n/netscratch`) via Globus instead. Both are available through Globus. 2. **Scratch** (`/n/netscratch`) is ideal for large, temporary data — it offers 50TB per lab with high I/O performance, but files are purged after 90 days and are not backed up [Scratch – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch/). 3. **Lab storage** (`/n/holylabs`) provides 4TB with indefinite retention (also not backed up) if you need longer-term storage [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/). 4. Once the data is in your lab directory or scratch, you can move or copy it as needed. ### Why not home directory? - Home directories are limited to **100 GB** and are not designed for large datasets or I/O-intensive work [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). - They are also not available via Globus. Would you like help setting up Globus access or choosing the best destination for your specific dataset?
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, use Globus — it's the recommended alternative to SSH/SCP specifically for very large data sets, and you can install Globus Connect Personal to make your own machine an endpoint. However, you cannot transfer into your home directory: home directories are not available through Globus (and never will be) because sharing them is a security vulnerability. Target a lab or scratch location instead.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

🤖 Assistant Message #6
Here's the short answer: **Yes, use Globus for large transfers** — it's the recommended tool for copying very large datasets to/from FASRC systems [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). **No, you cannot transfer directly into your home directory via Globus.** Home directories are explicitly excluded from Globus access for security reasons (to prevent accidental sharing of authentication keys/passwords) [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). ### Recommended approach for large datasets: 1. **Transfer to your lab directory** (`/n/holylabs`) or **scratch** (`/n/netscratch`) via Globus instead. Both are available through Globus. 2. **Scratch** (`/n/netscratch`) is ideal for large, temporary data — it offers 50TB per lab with high I/O performance, but files are purged after 90 days and are not backed up [Scratch – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch/). 3. **Lab storage** (`/n/holylabs`) provides 4TB with indefinite retention (also not backed up) if you need longer-term storage [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/). 4. Once the data is in your lab directory or scratch, you can move or copy it as needed. ### Why not home directory? - Home directories are limited to **100 GB** and are not designed for large datasets or I/O-intensive work [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). - They are also not available via Globus. Would you like help setting up Globus access or choosing the best destination for your specific dataset?

Question 17: question_17

❓ Question

I accidentally deleted some files in my home directory on Cannon. Can I get them back, and is there any situation where I can't?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rdiff-backup, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/KHARMA/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes, you can often recover accidentally deleted files from your home directory on Cannon because it is **regularly backed up** [Home and Lab directories](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). ### How to recover You can use `rdiff-backup` to restore files from previous backup snapshots. For example, to restore a specific file or directory, you would run a command like: ```bash rdiff-backup --restore-as-of <timestamp> /path/to/your/home/directory /path/to/restore/to ``` You can list available backup increments to see what timestamps are available. See the [rdiff-backup documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rdiff-backup/) for detailed commands and examples. ### When you might NOT be able to recover files While home directories are backed up, there are situations where recovery may not be possible: * **If the backup hasn't run yet** for the time period when the deletion occurred, you can only restore to the last successful backup. * **If you've exceeded your 100 GB home directory quota** and are unable to write files (including backup data), you may need to free up space first before restoring. In extreme cases, you might see errors like `rm: cannot remove ‘{filename}’: No space left on device` [Home directory full](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/). If you're unsure about your quota status or need help with the restore process, consider contacting FASRC support for assistance.
✅ Expected Answer
Usually yes: home directories have daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks, so you can recover recently deleted files from the .snapshot directory. The exception is if you delete your entire home directory — that also deletes the snapshots, and it is not recoverable.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Question 18: question_18

❓ Question

I want to run a short GPU job on Cannon. How do I request the GPU and make sure it has enough memory so it isn't killed?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise1/Python/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Python/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/R/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/heavyAI/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/AITools/ollama.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, here's how to request a GPU on Cannon and manage memory to avoid being killed: ### 1. Request the GPU Use the `--gres=gpu` flag in your SLURM command to request GPU resources. You'll also need to specify the GPU partition. Example for an interactive job: ```bash salloc --partition gpu_test --gres=gpu:1 --mem-per-cpu 2G -c 4 --time 01:00:00 ``` * `--partition gpu_test`: Specifies the GPU partition. * `--gres=gpu:1`: Requests 1 GPU. * `-c 4`: Requests 4 CPU cores. * `--time 01:00:00`: Sets the job time limit. For a batch script, you would use the equivalent `#SBATCH` directives: ```bash #SBATCH --partition gpu_test #SBATCH --gres=gpu:1 #SBATCH --mem-per-cpu 2G #SBATCH -c 4 #SBATCH --time 01:00:00 ``` ### 2. Prevent Memory Issues (OOM Kill) To ensure your job isn't killed due to memory limits, you must explicitly request enough memory using `--mem` or `--mem-per-cpu`: * **`--mem-per-cpu`**: Specifies the memory required per allocated CPU core. For example, `--mem-per-cpu 2G` requests 2 GB per core. * **`--mem`**: Specifies the total memory required for the entire job. If you don't specify memory, SLURM may use a default limit that could be too low for your GPU workload, leading to an Out-Of-Memory (OOM) kill. Adjust the memory values based on your specific application's needs. For more details on memory management, you can refer to the [Job Efficiency - Memory per Node](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise1/Python/README.md) and [Job Efficiency - Memory per CPU/core](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Python/README.md) guides.
✅ Expected Answer
Request the GPU with #SBATCH --gpus=1. Don't rely on the default memory — if you omit a memory request you only get 100 MB and the job will likely be killed, so set #SBATCH --mem=<N>G to a value your job needs. Submit to a GPU-capable partition and keep within its time limit (for short tests, use a test/short partition).
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

You should select the **POSTDOC/RESEARCHER** job title on the account request form. Pick POSTDOC/RESEARCHER. A Harvard faculty member who wants to sign up as an individual collaborator under another faculty member's cluster group should choose POSTDOC/RESEARCHER, because choosing FACULTY requires billing information and starts the new lab setup process (and FACULTY signups require vetting, so they take longer to process).

Question 23: question_23

❓ Question

What does someone with non-faculty PI rights have to supply at signup that an ordinary researcher does not?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, someone with **non-faculty PI rights** must supply **their own 33-digit billing string(s)** during signup, which an ordinary researcher (who signs up under a PI's group) does not need to provide. Specifically, the signup instructions state: > "If you have a non-faculty appointment but have PI rights with a supported affiliation and your own 33-digit billing string(s), you should select NON-FACULTY PI (your account and PI rights will need to be vetted before approval. FASRC may contact you for more information)." [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/) In contrast, ordinary researchers (POSTDOC/RESEARCHER, GRAD STUDENT, UNDERGRAD, or STAFF) sign up under an existing PI's cluster group and do not need to provide their own billing strings.
✅ Expected Answer
A NON-FACULTY PI must supply a 33-digit billing code at signup. Non-Faculty PI accounts also carry additional controls and privileges, and the signup requires vetting so it takes longer to process.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

🤖 Assistant Message #3
Based on the FASRC documentation, someone with **non-faculty PI rights** must supply **their own 33-digit billing string(s)** during signup, which an ordinary researcher (who signs up under a PI's group) does not need to provide. Specifically, the signup instructions state: > "If you have a non-faculty appointment but have PI rights with a supported affiliation and your own 33-digit billing string(s), you should select NON-FACULTY PI (your account and PI rights will need to be vetted before approval. FASRC may contact you for more information)." [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/) In contrast, ordinary researchers (POSTDOC/RESEARCHER, GRAD STUDENT, UNDERGRAD, or STAFF) sign up under an existing PI's cluster group and do not need to provide their own billing strings.

Question 24: question_24

❓ Question

Our lab has grant money and wants to buy extra compute nodes for the cluster. Can we just have them racked whenever we're ready?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/jupyter-notebook-server-on-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you can't just have nodes racked whenever you're ready. According to FASRC's documentation on Fairshare and Job Accounting: > "This is not an immediate solution to the problem as it takes time for hardware to be built and installed. That said once the hardware arrives your Share will be increased and your fairshare will improve commensurately. Please [contact](https://www.rc.fas.harvard.edu/about/contact) FASRC for more information if you wish to purchase hardware for the cluster." [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/) When you purchase hardware for the cluster, your lab's Share (which affects job priority via the fairshare system) is increased once the hardware is actually installed. However, there is a lead time for hardware to be built and installed, so it's not an immediate process. If you're interested in purchasing compute nodes, you should contact FASRC directly to discuss the process and timeline.
✅ Expected Answer
No. FASRC will discuss your computing needs, coordinate a quote, and install the new hardware, but as of the 2019 cluster refresh and the move to water-cooling, new nodes can be added into the cluster only at certain times of the year.

Question 25: question_25

❓ Question

If a Chan School PI sponsors an outside collaborator's cluster account, who ends up paying for that person's compute time, and what happens to their access if the PI leaves?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/offboarding

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the retrieved documentation, I can provide partial answers to your question, but some details appear to be missing from the indexed sources. ## Who pays for the outside collaborator's compute time? The indexed documentation does **not** contain specific information about how billing works when a Chan School PI sponsors an outside collaborator's account. The Chan School Q&A page ([Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health + FAS Research Computing Q&A](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa/)) covers account setup and support but doesn't address billing arrangements for outside collaborators. For Chan School-specific billing policies, you would need to contact: - The **HCSPH Bioinformatics Core** (located on the Longwood campus) for local support and consulting - **FASRC directly** at [rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu](mailto:rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu) ## What happens to access if the PI leaves? The offboarding policies ([Offboarding Policies and Procedures](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/offboarding/)) outline general procedures for when a PI leaves Harvard: 1. **The PI must inform FASRC** via email when they will be leaving the university. 2. **Research data ownership**: The University asserts ownership over research data for all projects conducted at the University. If the PI wants to take data with them, they need prior written approval from the Vice Provost for Research and an agreement from their new institution. 3. **For researchers (non-PIs)**: If they need to maintain an FASRC account after leaving, their PI or authorized lab member must email FASRC *prior* to departure to convert the account to an external account. The account will be closed when their appointment ends and Harvard email is closed. However, the indexed documentation doesn't specifically address what happens to **outside collaborators' accounts** when their Chan School PI sponsor leaves. This is likely a case-specific arrangement that would need to be discussed with FASRC directly. **Recommendation**: Contact FASRC at [rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu](mailto:rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu) or the HCSPH Bioinformatics Core for specific guidance on billing arrangements and account continuity for outside collaborators.
✅ Expected Answer
There is no up-front cost for the collaborator's account, but their cluster usage (in CPU hours) is applied to the sponsoring PI's lab group fairshare and billed to the school on a quarterly basis. The account must be requested as EXTERNAL and be approved by and attached to an existing HCSPH PI's FASRC account. Should the PI leave HCSPH, the collaborator's access also ends unless it is transferred to another PI's account and billing; it is the PI's responsibility to notify FASRC when the collaborator's access should be withdrawn.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa

❓ Question

Do Harvard Chan people still get charged that yearly per-person fee just to have a Research Computing account?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, I don't see specific information about a yearly per-person fee for Harvard Chan (HSPH) users to maintain a Research Computing account. The HSPH-FAS Research Computing documentation ([Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health + FAS Research Computing Q&A](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa/)) outlines the benefits of having an account, including access to the cluster, storage, and support, but doesn't mention a per-person account fee. However, the account qualifications page notes that "what arrangements your school or department has with FAS Research Computing" may vary ([Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/)), suggesting that billing structures can differ by school or department. For the most current and specific information about any fees, I'd recommend: - Contacting the [HCSPH Bioinformatics Core](http://bioinformatics.sph.harvard.edu/) which provides local support - Reaching out to FASRC directly at rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu - Checking with your PI or department administrator about any existing arrangements Would you like help with anything else regarding your Research Computing account?
✅ Expected Answer
No. The previous annual per-user charge for FAS Research Computing accounts is no longer used as of fiscal year 2019. Standard services — including accounts and access to the FASRC high-performance computing cluster — are effectively free to Harvard Chan School community members via the account of an eligible PI, because these FASRC computing costs are now covered by the school's overhead rate. Supplemental resources such as increased storage and VMs still involve additional charges.

Question 28: question_28

❓ Question

I'm on FASSE and want to run VS Code on a compute node. Can I just set up Remote-SSH to the compute node the way I do on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/vscode_remote_tunnel.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No—you can't use the same Remote-SSH setup on FASSE that works on Cannon. The SSH-based approach is documented specifically for **Cannon** and relies on Cannon's network topology (using `cannon` as the SSH proxy and `salloc` to reserve a node). FASSE has a different architecture, and compute nodes are not directly reachable via SSH from the login nodes in the same way. Instead, FASRC recommends using the **Remote Tunnel** approach on FASSE, which works by submitting a batch job to SLURM that launches the VS Code CLI tunnel inside the compute node. This is more resilient to network glitches and doesn't require direct SSH access to the compute node. Here's how to set it up on FASSE: 1. **Create a job script** (e.g., `vscode.job`) with something like: ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH -p <partition> # e.g., gpu, cpu, etc. #SBATCH --mem=8g #SBATCH --time=04:00:00 #SBATCH -c 1 set -o errexit -o nounset -o pipefail MY_SCRATCH=$(TMPDIR=/scratch mktemp -d) curl -Lk 'https://code.visualstudio.com/sha/download?build=stable&os=cli-alpine-x64' | tar -C $MY_SCRATCH -xzf - VSCODE_CLI_DISABLE_KEYCHAIN_ENCRYPT=1 $MY_SCRATCH/code tunnel user login --provider github $MY_SCRATCH/code tunnel --accept-server-license-terms --name fasstunnel ``` 2. **Submit the job** with `sbatch vscode.job` 3. **Connect from your local VS Code** using the Remote Tunnel extension, which will connect to the tunnel running inside your compute node. For full details, see the FASRC documentation on [VSCode Remote Development via SSH and Tunnel](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel/). Would you like help customizing the job script for your specific partition or resource needs?
✅ Expected Answer
No. You cannot use Remote SSH to connect your local VS Code to a FASSE compute node, because salloc is not permitted on FASSE for security reasons. Remote SSH to FASSE's login node does work. For a FASSE compute node, launching a Remote Tunnel as a batch job (Approach I) is your only option — and before doing that you must enable internet access by setting a network proxy (you are on the FASSE VPN realm, @fasse), including adding the same proxy in your local VS Code settings; remember to unset that proxy in VS Code when you are not on the FASSE VPN, or it will block your other non-FASSE VS Code remote sessions.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel
💬 Agent Messages (3)

🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document

Question 29: question_29

❓ Question

The VS Code Dev Containers extension keeps trying to talk to a Docker daemon on the cluster, which doesn't exist there. What do I have to change so it uses the container runtime FASRC actually provides?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman, slurm.schedmd.com/containers.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman, slurm.schedmd.com/containers.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The FASRC clusters use **Podman** (a daemonless container engine), not Docker. The Dev Containers extension expects a Docker daemon, so you need to tell it to use Podman's socket instead. Here's what to do: 1. **Start the Podman system service** on your compute node. In a VS Code terminal on the cluster, run: ```bash nohup podman system service -t 0 >/dev/null 2>&1 & ``` This creates a Unix socket at `/tmp/containers-user-$(id -u)/podman/podman.sock` that emulates the Docker Engine API [Podman – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman/). 2. **Set the `DOCKER_HOST` environment variable** so Docker-compatible tools (including Dev Containers) point to that socket: ```bash export DOCKER_HOST=unix:/$(podman info --format '{{.Host.RemoteSocket.Path}}') ``` You may also want: ```bash export CONTAINER_HOST=${DOCKER_HOST} ``` [Podman – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman/). 3. **Configure your `devcontainer.json`** to mount the Podman socket and disable BuildKit (which can cause issues with rootless Podman). At minimum, add: ```json "remoteUser": "root", "mounts": [ "source=/tmp/podman-run-<UID>/podman/podman.sock,target=/var/run/docker-host.sock,type=bind" ], "remoteEnv": { "DOCKER_BUILDKIT": "0" } ``` Replace `<UID>` with your actual user ID. See the full example at [VSCode Remote Development via SSH and Tunnel – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel/). **Key point:** You need to start the `podman system service` *on the compute node where your Dev Container runs*, not on the login node. If you're using a batch job to launch VS Code, you can add the `podman system service` command to your job script before starting VS Code.
✅ Expected Answer
Point the Dev Containers extension at Podman. In Code > Settings > Extensions > Dev Containers, change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Path' from 'docker' to 'podman', and change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Socket Path' from '/var/run/docker.sock' to '/tmp/podman-run-<uid>/podman/podman.sock', replacing <uid> with your FASRC user ID (obtained by running 'id -u' on the cluster). If you are using a local VS Code rather than an OOD Remote Desktop, you will need to revert these settings later if you use dev containers with a local Docker installation.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Question 30: question_30

❓ Question

I set up TF 2.16 on the cluster with the FASRC conda steps and now Keras blows up saying it has no backend. What environment variable am I supposed to export?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rstudio-server-vs-rstudio-desktop, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-and-rstudio, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/alphapose.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You need to export: ```bash export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" ``` This is required for Keras 3.0+, which is bundled with TensorFlow 2.16+. You can add it to your conda environment's activation script so it's set automatically every time you activate the environment: ```bash mkdir -p $CONDA_PREFIX/etc/conda/activate.d echo 'export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow"' >> $CONDA_PREFIX/etc/conda/activate.d/env_vars.sh ``` See the FASRC TensorFlow installation guide for full details [TensorFlow – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow/).
✅ Expected Answer
Export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow". Starting with version 2.16.1, TensorFlow includes Keras 3.0, so the Keras backend must be set to TF. FASRC's instructions include `export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow"` in both the GPU and CPU conda recipes, and prefix the singularity runs with it as well, e.g. `KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" singularity exec --nv tf2.16.1_gpu.simg python3`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow

Question 32: question_32

❓ Question

My PyTorch job on gpu_test sees a device but the card looks sliced up and I only get a fraction of it. What do I need to do differently on that partition?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/mmpose.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/pytorch/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On the `gpu_test` partition, GPUs are configured in **MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) mode**, which slices the card into smaller instances. PyTorch needs to be told which MIG instance to use via the `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` environment variable. Do this after starting your job: 1. **Find the MIG instance name:** ```bash nvidia-smi -L ``` 2. **Set `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` to that MIG instance:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b36b802-0ab0-5f37-af2d-ac23f40ef62d ``` (Replace with the actual string from your `nvidia-smi -L` output.) 3. **Or automate it in one line:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}') ``` This is only required on `gpu_test`; on the regular `gpu` partition it's not necessary [PyTorch – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch/).
✅ Expected Answer
gpu_test is currently the only FASRC partition with MIG (multi-instance GPU) mode enabled, so you must target a MIG instance explicitly. Get the GPU/MIG instance name with `nvidia-smi -L`, then set CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES to the MIG instance, e.g. `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b36b802-0ab0-5f37-af2d-ac23f40ef62d`, or automate it with `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}')`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch

🤖 Assistant Message #2

Do this after starting your job: 1. **Find the MIG instance name:** ```bash nvidia-smi -L ``` 2. **Set `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` to that MIG instance:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b36b802-0ab0-5f37-af2d-ac23f40ef62d ``` (Replace with the actual string from your `nvidia-smi -L` output.) 3. **Or automate it in one line:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}') ``` This is only required on `gpu_test`; on the regular `gpu` partition it's not necessary [PyTorch – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch/).

Question 33: question_33

❓ Question

Our CryoSPARC master keeps coming up bound to the wrong machine name so the workers can't reach it. Where is that hostname actually set?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/bashrc_additions.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/configure.sh, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The hostname that the CryoSPARC master advertises is set in the master's config file: ``` cryosparc_master/config.sh ``` Specifically, look for the `CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME` variable. For example: ```bash export CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME=holy_______.rc.fas.harvard.edu ``` You can find this documented in the FASRC CryoSPARC setup guide [github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md). To fix the issue: 1. Edit `cryosparc_master/config.sh` and set `CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME` to the correct hostname of the node where the master is running. 2. Restart the master so it picks up the new hostname: ```bash cryosparcm restart ``` If you also changed the port, you'll need to run `cryosparcm fixdbport` before restarting.
✅ Expected Answer
It is set in the CryoSPARC master config file, `cryosparc_master/config.sh`, which holds the license, the path to the MongoDB database, the master hostname, and the base TCP port. The hostname of the node running the master application is typically hardcoded in that config.sh; if it is left unset, CryoSPARC takes the hostname of the machine on which `cryosparcm start` is called. If these config.sh settings are not correct you will experience errors. Note also that the TCP ports of the individual component programs are not individually configurable — only the base port the user connects to is.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc

Question 34: question_34

❓ Question

Which processes does `cryosparcm start` bring up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/bashrc_additions.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/configure.sh, slurm.schedmd.com/cgroup_v2.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Running `cryosparcm start` brings up the following six applications: * **app** (CLI) * **command_core** * **command_rtp** * **command_vis** * **database** (MongoDB) * **webapp** These components communicate with each other over TCP. The base TCP port is configurable in `cryosparc_master/config.sh`, but individual ports for each component are not separately configurable [CryoSPARC – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc/).
✅ Expected Answer
Running `cryosparcm start` brings up six applications: app (cli), command_core, command_rtp, command_vis, database (MongoDB), and webapp. At the top level cryosparcm is a Supervisor-based shell script that manages them, and they mostly communicate with one another over TCP.

Question 35: question_35

❓ Question

Google finally approved my AlphaFold 3 request and sent me the weights as a .bin.zst download. Where am I supposed to unpack that on Cannon — can I just leave it in netscratch?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/alphafold_v3.def, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Good news — you **don't** need to unpack the `.bin.zst` file yourself at all. FASRC has already provided the AlphaFold 3 model parameters in a cluster-wide location: ``` /n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3 ``` You can point your `--model_dir` flag directly at that path when running AlphaFold 3. ### Key points: - **Do not** put the model weights in `netscratch` — the FASRC docs explicitly say to place them in a lab share, and FASRC has already done this for you in the shared location above [AlphaFold – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold/). - The databases (including model parameters) are stored on SSD as recommended by the developers. - You'll also need the AlphaFold 3 database at `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3`. ### Next steps: 1. Verify the model files are accessible in that shared directory. 2. Use the example SLURM scripts from the [FASRC User_Codes repo](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/) to run AlphaFold 3 in two steps: - **Step 1**: Data pipeline on a CPU partition - **Step 2**: Inference on a GPU partition Let me know if you need help with the SLURM job scripts or Singularity container setup!
✅ Expected Answer
Extract the file Google sends you with `unzstd file.bin.zst`, then place the resulting `file.bin` in a lab share — do NOT put it in netscratch. That location is what you pass as `--model_dir` when you run AlphaFold3.

Question 37: question_37

❓ Question

I copied a friend's sbatch script and stripped it down — it has no partition line, no memory line, and no time line. What does the scheduler actually hand me?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/JobArrays/Exercise_nonsequential_maxarray/array_script.sh, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/preempt.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_submit_plugins.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
If your `sbatch` script omits `#SBATCH --partition`, `#SBATCH --mem`, and `#SBATCH --time`, SLURM will fall back to its **cluster-wide defaults**. Here's what that typically means on FASRC: ### Partition SLURM assigns your job to the **default partition** configured in `slurm.conf`. On FASRC clusters, this is usually the general-purpose partition (e.g., `general` or `standard`). You can verify which partition your job landed in by running: ```bash squeue -u $USER ``` or ```bash sacct -j <jobid> --format=JobID,Partition ``` ### Memory The default memory limit is **very small** — typically **100 MB per node** — as noted in the FASRC documentation on [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). If your job exceeds this, it will be killed with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` state. You almost always need to explicitly request memory, e.g.: ```bash #SBATCH --mem=8G ``` ### Time The default time limit depends on the partition's configuration in `slurm.conf`. For the general partition on FASRC, the default is usually **24 hours**, but this can vary. You can check the partition's default time limit with: ```bash sinfo -p <partition_name> ``` Look for the `MAX` or `DEF` time columns. ### Summary | Setting | Default if omitted | |---------|-------------------| | Partition | Cluster default partition (e.g., `general`) | | Memory | ~100 MB per node (very likely too small) | | Time | Partition default (often 24 h, but verify) | **Recommendation:** Always explicitly specify `--partition`, `--mem`, and `--time` in your scripts to avoid surprises. The memory default is especially dangerous — jobs that need more than 100 MB will be silently killed.
✅ Expected Answer
FASRC's defaults when nothing is requested are: 1 core, 100 MB of memory, 0 GPUs, and the `serial_requeue` partition. Time is the exception — there is no default time limit, so you must always declare a time yourself. The 100 MB memory default is very likely to get your job killed, so you should always specify `--mem` or `--mem-per-cpu`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 38: question_38

❓ Question

I have about 12,000 near-identical runs to do. Can I submit them as one --array job, and does that eat into the number of jobs I'm allowed to have queued?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/resource_limits.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/rest_api.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/openapi_release_notes.html, slurm.schedmd.com/high_throughput.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, here's what you need to know about submitting 12,000 jobs as a job array: ### Yes, you can use a job array Slurm's job array feature lets you submit many similar jobs with a single `sbatch` command using the `--array` flag. Each array task is treated as a separate job element, but they all share a single job ID. ### Does it count against your queued job limit? **Yes, it does.** Each array task counts as an individual job toward your per-user job limits. The Slurm documentation notes that "job arrays offer an easy way to submit large numbers of jobs very quickly" and administrators should be "mindful about the value of MaxArraySize" for this reason [Slurm Workload Manager - Job Array Support](https://slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html). ### Key considerations for 12,000 jobs: 1. **MaxArraySize**: The default Slurm `MaxArraySize` is 1001, but can go up to 4,000,001. You'll need to check whether FASRC's cluster is configured to allow arrays of 12,000 elements. You can check your current limits with: ``` scontrol show config | grep MaxArraySize ``` 2. **Job limits**: Even though it's one submission, each of the 12,000 array tasks will count toward your per-user limits for: - Maximum number of queued jobs - Maximum number of running jobs - Maximum number of total jobs 3. **Practical advice**: If your per-user job limits are lower than 12,000, you may need to: - Submit in batches (e.g., multiple arrays of 1,000–2,000 jobs each) - Check your current limits with `scontrol show account <your-account>` or `sacct -u <username> -o MaxJobs,MaxSubmit` ### Recommended approach For 12,000 near-identical runs, consider: - Submitting in batches of ~1,000–2,000 jobs per array to stay within typical job limits - Using checkpointing so you can restart from where you left off if jobs fail - Using the scratch filesystem for I/O-intensive work rather than home directories [Cluster Storage – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/) Would you like help checking your specific job limits or constructing the `sbatch` command for your use case?
✅ Expected Answer
No — you must split it. The maximum array size is 10,000 (that cap applies to both the array index and the array size), so a single 12,000-element array is not allowed. And yes, it counts against your job cap: each array index counts as a single job for the purposes of the Maximum Number of Jobs per User, which is 10,100.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 39: question_39

❓ Question

My analysis takes about 3 hours every time on the shared partition, but when I run the exact same thing on serial_requeue it keeps getting killed for hitting the wall clock. What time should I be asking for there?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You should ask for **9 hours** on `serial_requeue`. The `serial_requeue` partition can place your job on a variety of hardware, including slower nodes. As a rule of thumb, there can be a factor of three variance in speed between nodes. So if your job takes 3 hours on most hardware, you should request 9 hours to account for potentially slower hosts [Job Efficiency and Optimization Best Practices](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices/). This ensures your job won't be killed for hitting the wall clock while waiting for or running on a slower node.
✅ Expected Answer
Ask for roughly 9 hours. `serial_requeue` (and `gpu_requeue`) are mosaic partitions made of a wide variety of hardware, so run times vary a lot depending on which host you land on. The rule of thumb is a factor of three variance in speed — so a job that takes 3 hours on most hardware should be given 9 hours on `serial_requeue`. Alternatively, use the `--constraint` option to pin the job to a specific hardware type so the runtime is predictable.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

Question 40: question_40

❓ Question

My PI bought the nodes in our lab's own partition, and we only ever run there. So why does our fairshare keep dropping — aren't we just using hardware we already paid for?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is a common point of confusion! The short answer is: **Yes, you are still charged fairshare even when running on your lab's private partition.** Here's why, based on FASRC documentation: ### How Fairshare Works Fairshare is a **global quantity** across the entire cluster. When you run jobs, your group is charged against its allocated "RawShares" regardless of which partition those jobs run on. This is explicitly stated in the FASRC Fairshare documentation: > "We give RawShares to everyone that can be used anywhere on the cluster since Fairshare is a global quantity. Hence a user is charged regardless of what partition they use." [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/) ### Why Your Fairshare Drops Even though your PI purchased the hardware for your private partition, your group's fairshare is calculated based on: 1. **Total resource usage** (cores × time, memory × time, GPUs × time) across **all** jobs 2. **How much of the cluster** your group has been granted via Shares When you run jobs on your private partition, those jobs still consume TRES (Trackable RESources) and are charged against your group's account. If your usage exceeds your allocated share, your fairshare score drops. ### What Can Help Once the hardware arrives and is installed, your group's **RawShares** should be increased commensurately with the hardware in your partition, which should improve your fairshare over time. If you haven't already, you can [contact FASRC](https://www.rc.fas.harvard.edu/about/contact) for more information about how your share was calculated and what to expect once the hardware is fully integrated. Would you like help checking your current fairshare status or understanding how to calculate your expected usage?
✅ Expected Answer
Because Fairshare is a global quantity on Cannon, not a per-partition one: RawShares are granted to everyone and can be used anywhere on the cluster, so a user is charged regardless of which partition they run in. Groups that own private partitions are granted RawShares equivalent to the hardware in that partition, and that grant exactly offsets the usage of the partition — so it nets out. The upside of this design is flexibility: because the Share is global, a group can leave its own partition idle and spend its shares elsewhere on the cluster instead.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 41: question_41

❓ Question

Last week a bunch of our jobs died from node failures and we cancelled several more that were misconfigured. Does that wasted compute still count against our lab's score, and how long until it stops hurting us?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/job_state_codes.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
I don't have specific information in the retrieved sources about how FASRC calculates or resets lab "scores" or quotas in the context of wasted compute from node failures or cancelled jobs. What I can confirm from the documentation is that jobs which die from `NODE_FAILURE` are automatically requeued by the scheduler [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/). For details on how compute usage is tracked, whether failed or cancelled jobs count against your lab's allocation, and any grace periods or reset windows, you'll want to contact RCHelp directly at [https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) with your job IDs and details about the failures.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, it still counts. RawUsage is the sum of all usage including failed jobs, jobs that are requeued, and jobs that ran on nodes that failed — FASRC counts it because it is the user's responsibility to use the allocated time effectively even when it is cut short. That usage does decay, though: RawUsage is attenuated by the cluster's half-life, currently 3 days. Work done in the last 3 days counts at full cost, work done 6 days ago costs half, work done 9 days ago one fourth, and so on. To limit the damage from failures, FASRC recommends testing/verifying code before running and enabling checkpointing so jobs can restart where they left off.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare
💬 Agent Messages (2)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: Last week a bunch of our jobs died from node failures and we cancelled several more that were misconfigured. Does that wasted compute still count against our lab's score, and how long until it stops hurting us?
🤖 Assistant Message #2
I don't have specific information in the retrieved sources about how FASRC calculates or resets lab "scores" or quotas in the context of wasted compute from node failures or cancelled jobs. What I can confirm from the documentation is that jobs which die from `NODE_FAILURE` are automatically requeued by the scheduler [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/). For details on how compute usage is tracked, whether failed or cancelled jobs count against your lab's allocation, and any grace periods or reset windows, you'll want to contact RCHelp directly at [https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) with your job IDs and details about the failures.

❓ Question

I'm a master's student and my advisor wants me to run analyses on the Nielsen consumer panel data. Can I be added to the agreement?

Undergraduates and master's students are not eligible for access. If you're a master's student, you may need to work with your advisor or a PhD student who has access to conduct the analysis on your behalf.

That researcher can be a Ph.D. student or a postdoc who has a tenure-track faculty member registered on the site — but the data is explicitly not available to undergraduates or master's students. Relatedly, under the Data Access and Confidentiality Agreement the Researcher may disclose the Data only to PhD-seeking students of Harvard working under their supervision on the Research Project.

Question 44: question_44

❓ Question

I want to spin up a few hundred cores on Cannon to crawl a public website faster. It's for academic research — any issue with that?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/common-pitfalls, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-security-levels, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasrc-cluster-storage-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasrc-ai-facilitation

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — there are significant policy and practical issues with running large-scale web crawling on the FASRC clusters. ### FASRC policy: scraping on the cluster is strongly discouraged FASRC's Web Scraping Policy states that they **"highly discourage using the cluster itself to scrape data"** because the cluster's size and ease of parallelization make it "easily weaponized" and your actions could have consequences for other researchers. They explicitly recommend that you **"seek another avenue for data acquisition first"** and that you **"contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity using the FASRC cluster."** The preferred approach is to do the scraping elsewhere and then bring the data to the cluster for processing. [Web Scraping Policy – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy) ### Other concerns - **Target site policies**: Even for academic research, you are bound by the target website's terms of service, robots.txt, and any explicit scraping restrictions. Being an academic does not exempt you from those policies. - **Legal risk**: Aggressive or unauthorized scraping can lead to IP blacklisting and even legal action against Harvard. - **Resource impact**: Requesting "a few hundred cores" for crawling could also raise concerns about job efficiency and fair use of cluster resources, especially if the workload doesn't scale well or ties up nodes for extended periods. ### Recommended next steps 1. **Contact FASRC** before starting any scraping activity on the cluster to discuss your use case and get guidance. 2. **Consider doing the scraping off-cluster** (e.g., on your own infrastructure or a cloud service) and then transfer the data to FASRC for analysis. 3. **Review the target site's policies** (robots.txt, terms of service) and consult Harvard's Office of the General Counsel or Office of the Vice Provost for Research if you're unsure about compliance. Would you like help with alternative approaches or guidance on submitting a job to FASRC once you have your data?
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, several. FASRC highly discourages using the cluster itself to scrape: because of its size and ease of parallelization, the cluster is easily weaponized and your actions could have consequences for other researchers, so you should seek another avenue for data acquisition first. You must contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity using the FASRC cluster. It is highly preferable that you do the scraping elsewhere and then bring the data to the cluster for processing — and if the data is sensitive, confidential, contains human data, or it is unclear, doing it elsewhere is a requirement. Highly parallelized scraping is specifically called out as risking blacklisting of the cluster's or the university's IP range, which would harm other users. Being an academic does not exempt you from a site's usage policies.
