Question 45: question_45

❓ Question

I logged in to Globus and picked the Harvard FAS RC Holyoke collection, but I can't browse into my home directory and the Everyone folder in my lab share doesn't show up either. Is my account misconfigured?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/academic-use

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Your account is not misconfigured — this is expected behavior. Here's what's happening: **Home directories are not available via Globus.** As stated in the documentation, home directories cannot be shared via Globus under any circumstances because doing so creates a security vulnerability (authentication keys/passwords may be shared inadvertently) [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). **The "Everyone" folder is not available via Globus either.** Globus can only see folders inside `Lab` and `Users/$USER`. The `Everyone` and `Transfer` directories are not available through Globus and are intended only for internal sharing on the cluster [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). **What you *can* access via Globus:** - Any folders inside your lab's `Lab` directory - The `Users/$USER` directory (though this has been deprecated as of 2025) If you find that your lab share is not visible in the Globus collection or the expected subdirectories don't exist, you should contact FASRC support.
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is misconfigured — both are expected. Home directories are not and will not be added to Globus under any circumstances, because sharing home directories creates a security vulnerability (authentication keys/passwords could be shared inadvertently). And within a lab share, Globus can only see the `Lab` and `Users/$USER` folders — anything outside those, such as `Everyone` and `Transfer`, is not available through Globus (note the `Users` folder has been deprecated as of 2025). Also be aware symlinks do not work in Globus. If your lab share genuinely lacks a `Lab` directory or is not visible at all in the Holyoke/Boston collections, contact FASRC.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Question 46: question_46

❓ Question

I need to move a directory holding roughly half a million small output files off the cluster with Globus. What's the recommended way to package that up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
For a directory with ~500,000 small files, the recommended approach is to **create one or more `tar` archives** before transferring via Globus. Transferring hundreds of thousands of individual files through Globus is extremely slow and inefficient. Here's the recommended workflow: ### 1. Create a file list (optional but useful) ```bash cd /path/to/your/data find mydir/ -type f > file-list.txt ``` ### 2. Create a checksum file for integrity verification ```bash find mydir/ -type f -print0 | xargs --null -P $(nproc) shasum | sort > mydir.shasum ``` ### 3. Create a tar archive ```bash tar -cf mydir.tar mydir/ ``` ### 4. (Optional) Capture ACLs if needed ```bash getfacl -R mydir/ > mydir.acl ``` ### Key recommendations: - **Break large datasets into smaller archives** (e.g., 50–200 GB each) rather than one massive tar file. This reduces the risk of total data loss from corruption in any single archive [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/). - **Keep checksums** so you can verify integrity after transfer: `sha256sum -c mydir.shasum` - **Transfer the tar file(s) via Globus** instead of the individual files. This is dramatically faster and more reliable. Once transferred, the recipient can extract with: ```bash tar -xf mydir.tar ``` Would you like help with the Globus transfer setup or any other step?
✅ Expected Answer
Tar the directory into subset files first. FASRC recommends that your tar files range in size from 1-100 GiB — that size range transfers more quickly (especially if the transfer is interrupted) and is smaller to retrieve if it ever needs to come back from Tape. If you leave hundreds or thousands of files in a single directory, Globus will go into an 'endpoint is too busy' state and your job will timeout, restart, timeout, etc. FASRC also recommends reading their 'Tips for using tar to archive data' page before tarring.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer
💬 Agent Messages (4)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: I need to move a directory holding roughly half a million small output files off the cluster with Globus. What's the recommended way to package that up?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: Globus transfer many small files tar archive packaging recommendation
🛠️ Tool Call #3: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.

Question 47: question_47

❓ Question

I ran shasum on a folder before I tarred it up, and when I extracted the archive later and hashed it again the values don't match, even though every file looks fine. What am I doing wrong?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/7zip

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The most common reason for this is that **the checksum was generated before the `tar` command finished**, or the directory was modified between the two operations. As noted in the FASRC documentation on archiving: > "If the data in the directory is modified after you've run the checksum and before you've tar'd it, then the checksum will no longer match later when un-tar and compare later. If you need to tar an active filesystem, then checksum'ing will not be useful to you." [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/) Here are the key things to check: 1. **Timing**: Make sure the checksum was generated *after* all writes to the directory were complete and *before* you started the `tar` command. If the directory is "active" (files are still being written), the checksum won't be reliable. 2. **Hidden files**: `shasum` on a directory path (e.g., `shasum mydir/`) may not recursively hash all files. Use `find` to ensure you're hashing every file: ```bash find mydir/ -type f -print0 | xargs --null shasum | sort > checksums.shasum ``` 3. **File metadata**: `shasum` only hashes file *contents*, not metadata like permissions, ownership, timestamps, or ACLs. If you need to verify those too, you'd need to capture that separately (e.g., with `getfacl`). 4. **Extraction differences**: When you extract, make sure you're in the same working directory structure. Extracting into a different parent directory or with different options (e.g., `tar -xvf` vs `tar -xvf --strip-components=1`) can change relative paths, which would cause a mismatch if you're comparing paths in the checksum file. **Recommended workflow** (from the FASRC guide): 1. Generate the checksum file first: `find mydir/ -type f -print0 | xargs --null -P $(nproc) shasum | sort > mydir.shasum` 2. Then create the tar archive. 3. After extraction, verify with: `shasum -c mydir.shasum` This way you're comparing file contents directly, not the directory hash itself.
✅ Expected Answer
The checksum depends on the path you hand to find, so the same files hashed two different ways produce two different values. FASRC's guidance is to always do it the exact same way: cd to the directory *above* the one you are archiving (e.g. /n/mypath/scans) and then run find against the relative directory name (myscans) — not the full path. Two other things will break the comparison: (1) modifying the data after you took the checksum but before you tarred it (a checksum is useless on an active filesystem), and (2) storing the checksum file itself inside the tar, since its presence changes the checksum unless you remove it after un-tarring and before re-running.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving
💬 Agent Messages (3)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: I ran shasum on a folder before I tarred it up, and when I extracted the archive later and hashed it again the values don't match, even though every file looks fine. What am I doing wrong?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.

Question 48: question_48

❓ Question

RC made a tar of my project with Starfish. I copied it to netscratch and extracted it there, but instead of my ProjectX folder I got this weird nested tree of empty directories with ProjectX way down at the bottom. Is the archive corrupt?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The archive is **not corrupt**. This is expected behavior when extracting a tar file created by Starfish. Starfish captures the **entire absolute path** inside the tar file. So when you extract it into a directory (e.g., `/n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/`), it recreates that full path underneath it. Your `ProjectX` folder ends up nested deep inside, like: ``` /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX ``` ### How to fix it You can extract the archive directly into the parent directory you want, or move the data up afterward. For example: ```bash # Extract into a specific directory, stripping the leading path tar -xf mystarfishtar.tar -C /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/ ``` This will place the contents of the archive into `/n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/` while preserving the internal path structure. If you want `ProjectX` to appear directly in your current directory, you can move it up: ```bash mv /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/ ``` Or, if you only need temporary access, you can leave it as-is and just navigate to the nested path. For more details, see [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving).
✅ Expected Answer
The archive is fine. Starfish tars in place and captures the entire path inside the tar file, so extracting recreates that whole path underneath your output directory. Extracting a Starfish tar of /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX into /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp gives you /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX. Move the ProjectX directory to where you actually want it, then remove the leftover empty n/holylabs/jharvard_lab tree. Use plain `rm` rather than `rm -rf`, so it errors out if other files remain or you typed the wrong path.

Question 49: question_49

❓ Question

I already pushed a bunch of archives into our lab's tape area on Globus and then realized I named them badly. Can I just rename them, or move a couple into a different folder to tidy things up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Unfortunately, **you cannot rename or move files on tape storage via Globus**. Here's why: - The tape storage uses a disk-based staging/buffer area that is available via Globus, but once data is written, it's migrated to tape [Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/). - There is no way to restrict permissions on individual folders within your lab tape collection — read/write access applies to the whole lab collection on Globus [Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/). - The underlying filesystem is not owned by FASRC, so we cannot set permissions or perform file operations like rename/move [Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/). **What you can do:** 1. If the files are still in the staging area and haven't been migrated to tape yet, you might be able to delete and re-upload with correct names (but this depends on your allocation's policies). 2. If they're already on tape, you'll need to work with the files as-is, or request assistance from FASRC support at rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu. I'd recommend contacting rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu to ask whether the files are still in the staging area and if deletion/re-upload is possible in your case.
✅ Expected Answer
No — do not delete, move, or rename data once it has been transferred to the tape buffer. The Globus buffer gives you write access to upload data destined for tape, and all changes within your lab's Globus collection are automatically picked up by the tape robot. Deletions on the Globus buffer may result in permanent deletion on tape, and deleting files that have already been placed on tape removes the 'link' and makes retrieval impossible. Mass deletions, moves, and file renaming require cumbersome manual intervention. Name files appropriately before you transfer them, and work with your PI or lab data manager on your lab's tape storage protocols.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Question 50: question_50

❓ Question

Our PI wants to let a postdoc read and write only one project folder inside our lab's tape collection on Globus, not the rest of it. How do we set that up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**Summary of Attempt:** The agent attempted to retrieve specific documentation on configuring Globus permissions for subdirectories within a shared lab collection. It performed multiple hybrid vector searches targeting keywords like "Globus share folder permissions," "specific directory only," and "postdoc" access. The retrieved context provided general information about Globus file transfer, tape system access, and POSIX permissions, but did not contain a specific, step-by-step guide for restricting Globus share access to a single subfolder within a larger tape collection. **Answer:** To allow a postdoc read/write access to only one project folder within your lab’s Globus share (without accessing the rest of the collection), you should use **Globus Collections and Share Permissions** rather than relying solely on POSIX file permissions. Here is the recommended approach: 1. **Create a Specific Share for the Subfolder:** * Log in to the Globus Web App. * Navigate to the **Data Manager** or **Shares** section. * Create a new **Share** that points specifically to the **single project folder** (subdirectory) you want to share, rather than sharing the entire parent directory. * *Note:* If your lab’s Globus collection is set up such that the entire directory structure is exposed, you may need to create a separate "Share" object that maps only to that specific path. 2. **Grant Permissions to the Postdoc:** * Once the share is created, invite the postdoc’s Globus email address to this specific share. * Set their permission level to **Read/Write** (or "Manage" if they need to create new folders). * Because the share is scoped only to that specific subfolder, the postdoc will only see and be able to access that folder via their Globus interface. They will not see the rest of the lab’s collection. 3. **Alternative: POSIX Permissions (If Globus Share Scoping is Not Possible):** * If your Globus setup does not allow creating granular shares for subdirectories (i.e., the entire parent directory is shared), you must rely on **POSIX file permissions**. * Change the ownership or group of the specific project folder to a group that includes only the PI and the postdoc. * Set the folder permissions to `770` (rwx for owner and group, no access for others). * Ensure the postdoc is added to that group. * *Warning:* This method is less robust in Globus if the parent directory is shared broadly, as the postdoc might still *see* other folders but will get "Permission Denied" errors when trying to access them. The Globus Share method (Step 1) is preferred for a cleaner user experience. **Important Note:** This run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50. The above answer is based on standard Globus best practices and the general context retrieved, but for lab-specific configurations, please consult your institution’s Globus administrator or the Harvard FAS RC documentation linked in the search results.
✅ Expected Answer
You cannot. There is no way to restrict permissions on individual folders within your lab tape collection — read/write access applies to the whole lab collection on Globus. The underlying filesystem is not owned by FASRC, so FASRC cannot set those permissions; everyone with access is essentially a guest on the NESE tape system. (The PI and General Manager can add lab members to the collection, but only to all of it.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

On Cannon I just do salloc to grab a shell on a compute node, but on FASSE it doesn't work. How am I supposed to get an interactive session there?

🎯 Retrieval Check

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, I can see that FASSE is a separate FASRC cluster from Cannon, but I don't have specific documentation in my indexed sources detailing the exact interactive session workflow differences between the two clusters. However, here's what I can tell you from the sources: - FASSE has its own login infrastructure and Open OnDemand instance at [https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/](https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) [FASSE OOD](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters/) - FASSE uses a different VPN realm (`@fasse`) compared to Cannon's `@fasrc` [About Usernames](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames/) **What to try:** 1. **Check which login node you're connecting to** — make sure you're actually connecting to FASSE login nodes (e.g., `fasse` or `fasseXXX.rc.fas.harvard.edu`) rather than Cannon nodes. 2. **Try `srun` instead of `salloc`** — On some clusters, `srun --pty` is the preferred way to get an interactive shell: ```bash srun --pty --time=01:00:00 --mem=4G /bin/bash ``` 3. **Check available partitions** on FASSE with: ```bash sinfo ``` If `salloc` truly doesn't work on FASSE, it may be that the cluster has different Slurm configuration or requires specific partition flags. I'd recommend checking the [FASSE documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse/) or contacting FASRC support for cluster-specific guidance.
✅ Expected Answer
salloc is deliberately unavailable on FASSE — it is disabled for security reasons, and FASSE does not allow interactive jobs from the command line at all. For interactive work you use the FASSE Open OnDemand (OOD) / VDI virtual desktop at https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu, reachable in your browser while connected to the FASRC VPN on the @fasse realm (note @fasse, not the @fasrc realm you use for Cannon). OOD gives you pre-built apps and interactive command-line access in a desktop-like environment. SSH to fasselogin.rc.fas.harvard.edu remains available for batch work.

Question 53: question_53

❓ Question

A data provider will only let me download their dataset if I give them an IP address to add to their allowlist. What address do they see when I pull the data from a FASSE compute node?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/network.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you download data from a FASSE compute node, the external server will see the IP address of Harvard's proxy server (**rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu**), not the compute node's private IP. This is because FASSE nodes are behind a firewall and must route external internet traffic through the proxy at `rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu:3128` [FASSE Proxy Settings](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings). **What you should do:** 1. **Contact FASRC** to ask for the specific public IP address(es) of the proxy that you can provide to the data provider for their allowlist. 2. Alternatively, ask the data provider if they can allowlist the entire Harvard network range, or if they have an alternative method for granting access (e.g., an API key or authentication token). **Important note:** FASSE's proxy does not allow *all* traffic, only what is necessary for research work [FASSE Proxy Settings](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings). If the data provider's server is blocked by the proxy, you may need to work with FASRC to get it whitelisted on their end as well.
✅ Expected Answer
140.247.232.228. All traffic to the Internet from FASSE — VPN, login, compute, or fasseood — goes out through the FASRC proxy (rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu), which currently has that external IP address. It is subject to change, so you can confirm it by running `curl icanhazip.com` from the node you will actually be working on.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings

🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: A data provider will only let me download their dataset if I give them an IP address to add to their allowlist. What address do they see when I pull the data from a FASSE compute node?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.

Question 55: question_55

❓ Question

Our level 3 project storage doesn't show up as a collection in Globus at all. What do we have to do to get it added?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, **data security level 3 (DSL3) storage shares are not automatically added to Globus** [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). To get your DSL3 project storage added, you need to **contact FASRC** directly. The docs state: > "If you find that your lab share is not seen by Globus or these sub directories don't exist in your lab's share, in the Holyoke or Boston collections, please contact us." Specifically, you would use either: * **"Harvard FAS RC Holyoke Secure"** — for DSL3 lab shares in the Holyoke data center * **"Harvard FAS RC Boston Secure"** — for DSL3 lab shares in the Boston data center Since DSL3 shares require special handling for security reasons, they aren't auto-provisioned like lower-security shares. Reach out to FASRC support to request the addition of your DSL3 storage to Globus.
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is broken: DSL 3 / FASSE storage is intentionally not included in Globus by default. To have it exposed, first review the governing DUA — Globus makes data available over the Internet without a VPN or private network, so a DUA requiring VPN/private-network access for all access to the data would not be Globus-compliant (consult your School Security Officer if unsure). Then submit a ticket to rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu that (1) includes the path to the project to add (e.g. /n/piname_project_l3), (2) states that the PI attests Globus is compliant with any contracts/DUAs governing the data, and (3) is either sent by the PI or receives a direct reply from the PI confirming it.

Question 56: question_56

❓ Question

How much CPU and RAM am I actually allowed on the FASRC login server, and is there a cap on how many terminal windows I can have open at once?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/Large_Data_Processing_R/parallel_computation/R_parLapply_vdi.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, I can answer part of your question, but the specific numeric limits aren't detailed in the indexed sources. ### CPU and RAM on Login Nodes FASRC does **not** publish explicit per-user CPU or RAM limits for the login nodes in the available documentation. Instead, the policy is behavioral: - The login nodes (holylogin## and boslogin##) are a **shared, multi-user resource** and must be kept free of significant computation [Cluster Customs and Responsibilities](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities/). - You should **not** run jobs or heavy applications (e.g., MATLAB, Mathematica) on the login server. Use an interactive SLURM session or a batch job instead [Command line access with Terminal (login nodes)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access/). - Running even "modestly memory-intensive" work on login nodes is discouraged because it impacts other users [Cluster Customs and Responsibilities](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities/). In practice, FASRC monitors login node usage and may terminate processes that consume excessive resources, but the exact thresholds are not documented publicly. ### Terminal Windows The documentation does **not** specify a hard cap on the number of terminal sessions or SSH connections you can have open simultaneously. However, since the login nodes are shared resources, opening many sessions that consume CPU or memory could trigger administrative intervention. ### Recommendation If you need to know your specific resource limits or if you're concerned about hitting implicit thresholds, I recommend contacting FASRC support directly for guidance tailored to your account.
✅ Expected Answer
Each login-node session is limited to 1 core and 8GB of memory, and you may have at most 5 sessions per user. Users who abuse the login nodes may have their sessions terminated, and the login nodes are rebooted during monthly maintenance to clear out stale sessions. If you need more than 5 sessions, switch to batch jobs, use Open OnDemand (which gives you a desktop with multiple terminals on a dedicated compute node), or use screen/tmux to multiplex one session.

Question 57: question_57

❓ Question

I ran ssh-copy-id and my key works great when I'm in the office, but from my apartment it still makes me punch in the 6-digit code every single time. Did the key not install properly?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-to-a-compute-node, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-key-error, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/using-ssh-controlmaster-for-single-sign-on, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The agent attempted to diagnose why SSH two-factor authentication (2FA) is still prompting for a 6-digit code from your apartment despite using `ssh-copy-id`. It searched the knowledge base for SSH key installation issues, 2FA/MFA configurations, and SSH connection behaviors (including ControlMaster and key fingerprint errors). **Answer:** The key was likely installed correctly, as it works in the office. The issue is almost certainly due to **SSH configuration differences** between your office and apartment environments, specifically regarding how the SSH client handles authentication agents or connection sharing. 1. **SSH Agent Forwarding:** In the office, you might be using an SSH agent that caches your key or handles the 2FA prompt once per session. At home, if you are not running an SSH agent or if the agent isn't forwarding correctly, the SSH client may be forced to re-authenticate (including the 2FA step) for every connection. 2. **SSH ControlMaster (Connection Sharing):** As noted in the retrieved documents, `ControlMaster` allows multiple sessions over a single connection. If your office setup uses this to maintain a persistent connection, you won't be prompted repeatedly. At home, if this isn't configured in your `~/.ssh/config`, each new SSH session triggers a full authentication cycle, including the 2FA prompt. 3. **Host Key Changes:** If the office and apartment networks route through different gateways or if the server's host key fingerprint has changed (as mentioned in the "SSH key error, DNS spoofing message" doc), SSH may reject the cached identity or require re-verification, though this usually results in an error rather than a 2FA prompt. **Recommendation:** Check your `~/.ssh/config` file at home to ensure it mirrors your office configuration, particularly any `ControlMaster` or `ForwardAgent` settings. You can also try adding `AddKeysToAgent yes` to your SSH config to have the key cached in the agent after the first 2FA prompt. **Note:** This run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50, so further deep-dive troubleshooting into specific config file syntax was not completed.
✅ Expected Answer
The key is fine — this is by design. Passwordless (ssh-key) authentication is only permitted to the Cannon login nodes from Harvard networks, including Harvard/FASRC VPNs. If you connect from an external network you are still required to provide two-factor authentication regardless of your key. So either connect to the VPN from home, or accept the 2FA prompt. (On FASSE, passwordless ssh is permitted from the FASSE VPN only.) You can confirm the key installed by checking that it is present in ~/.ssh/authorized_keys on the cluster.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

❓ Question

I'm an IQSS researcher and my survey data is classified Level 2. Which cluster am I supposed to be on, and what exactly goes in the VPN box versus the cluster login box?

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Since your survey data is classified **Level 2**, you are supposed to be on the **Cannon** cluster. Cannon is specifically designated as the Faculty of Arts and Sciences research computing cluster for users with Data Security Level 2 data [IQSS Cannon Quickstart Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide). Regarding the login boxes: 1. **VPN Box:** This is where you enter the Cisco AnyConnect server address, which is **vpn.rc.fas.harvard.edu**. You use this to establish a secure connection to the Harvard network before accessing the cluster. 2. **Cluster Login Box:** Once connected via VPN, you log into the Cannon cluster using your standard FASRC credentials (username and password/key). If you were handling **Level 3** data, you would instead need to use the **FASSE** (FAS Secure Environment) cluster and log in with your username in the format `username@fasse` [IQSS FASSE Quickstart Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasse-quickstart-guide).
✅ Expected Answer
Level 2 data belongs on Cannon, the FAS research computing cluster for Data Security Level 2 (only Level 3 data must move to the FASSE secure environment). To get on: in the Cisco AnyConnect VPN client use server address vpn.rc.fas.harvard.edu and enter your FASRC username in the format username@fasrc, your FASRC password, and your FASRC 2FA token — note this is NOT the code associated with your HarvardKey account. Then log into the Cannon cluster at https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu using just your FASRC username WITHOUT the '@fasrc', plus your FASRC password.

Question 59: question_59

❓ Question

My pandas script keeps blowing up with a memory error because the file is bigger than the node's RAM. Does FASRC want me to rewrite it, or can I keep using pandas somehow?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/Example2/run.sbatch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/pbdMPI/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/R/Example2/run.sbatch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/pbdMPI/run_scatter.sbatch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise1/Python/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You don’t need to abandon pandas entirely — you just need to process the data in chunks so it fits in memory. Pandas has built-in support for this via the `chunksize` parameter in `pd.read_csv()` (and similar functions). ### Quick fix: Use `chunksize` Instead of loading the whole file at once: ```python import pandas as pd # Process in chunks of 100,000 rows for chunk in pd.read_csv('large_file.csv', chunksize=100000): # Process each chunk result = process(chunk) # Accumulate or write results incrementally ``` This way, only one chunk lives in memory at a time. ### Other options if you still hit memory limits: 1. **Request more memory in your SLURM job** — by default, FASRC jobs get only 100 MB. Add `--mem=XXG` to your `sbatch` script to request more RAM [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). 2. **Use `dask`** — Dask provides a pandas-like API that works on larger-than-memory datasets by parallelizing across cores or nodes. 3. **Use `polars` or `pyarrow`** — These libraries are often more memory-efficient than pandas for reading and processing large files. 4. **Downcast data types** — Use `dtype` in `read_csv()` to load columns as smaller types (e.g., `float32` instead of `float64`, `category` for strings with few unique values). If you share your current approach, I can suggest a more tailored fix.
✅ Expected Answer
Both options exist, and which one FASRC recommends depends on scale. Start with chunking. For moderately large datasets, Dask can enhance Pandas by parallelizing operations — you can convert a Pandas DataFrame to a Dask DataFrame with dd.from_pandas() and keep most of your existing code. But for very large datasets that exceed memory constraints, using Dask alone as a substitute for Pandas is the more effective solution: Dask is a drop-in replacement with a familiar DataFrame API that works lazily, breaking work into smaller tasks that only execute when you call .compute(). Separately, switching from CSV to a columnar format like Parquet (e.g. df.to_parquet('output_data.parquet', compression='snappy')) greatly improves I/O performance.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

🛠️ Tool Call #2: search_vectorstore_hybrid

🛠️ Tool Call #3: search_vectorstore_hybrid

Question 60: question_60

❓ Question

I switched to the newest Intel module on the cluster and now the compiler command I've always used doesn't exist anymore. What am I supposed to call to build my C file now?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fortran

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, when you load the newest Intel module (like `intel/23.0.0-fasrc01`), the compiler command has changed from `icc` to **`icx`** for C compilation. You can verify this by running: ```bash $ which icx ``` This will show you the full path to the new Intel oneAPI C compiler. For example, to compile a C file: ```bash $ icx your_file.c -o your_program ``` The new Intel oneAPI modules use `icx` (Intel C compiler) instead of the older `icc` command. If you need to check what compiler commands are available after loading a module, you can use `which` to see the full path. Would you like help with any specific compilation flags or options for the new compiler?
✅ Expected Answer
Intel renamed the compiler binaries at version 24.0.0. For Intel modules below 24.0.0 the commands are icc (C), ifortran (Fortran), and icpc (C++). For Intel 24.0.0 and above they are icx (C), ifx (Fortran), and icpx (C++). So with a newer Intel module you must invoke icx instead of icc. (You can list the available Intel compilers with `module spider intel` and load a specific one, e.g. `module load intel/23.0.0-fasrc01`.)

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Question 61: question_61

❓ Question

Is it fine to build my C code right after I ssh in, and should I just put the gcc line at the top of my job script so it's always fresh?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, it's not fine to build your C code right after you SSH in, and you shouldn't put the `gcc` line at the top of your job script to compile on login nodes. Here's why: 1. **Don't compile on login nodes**: FASRC recommends jumping to a compute node to compile C code instead of using a login node. Compilation can take from a few seconds to a minute depending on your code's complexity, and doing this on login nodes wastes shared resources that all users need for interactive work [C Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language). 2. **Don't put gcc in job scripts for compilation**: While you should put module loads in your job scripts (not in your `.bashrc`), the compilation step itself should be done on a compute node. You can request an interactive job on a compute node to compile your code, or better yet, use the `test` partition for compilation and testing [Cpp, C++ Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language). **Best practice**: Request an interactive job on a compute node (using `salloc` or `srun`) to compile your C code, then run your actual job script on compute nodes as well. This keeps login nodes fast for everyone and ensures you're using the right resources for compilation.
✅ Expected Answer
No on both counts. FASRC recommends jumping to a compute node to compile a C program (compilation can take a few seconds to a minute depending on the code's complexity), and specifically recommends using the `test` partition to compile and test the program before running the production job. Best practice is to compile the code separately and then use the resulting executable in your sbatch script — avoid putting the compilation command inside the sbatch script, because that recompiles the program on every submission. If you change the source, recompile separately and then resubmit the production batch job.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language
💬 Agent Messages (2)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: Is it fine to build my C code right after I ssh in, and should I just put the gcc line at the top of my job script so it's always fresh?
🤖 Assistant Message #2

Here's why: 1. **Don't compile on login nodes**: FASRC recommends jumping to a compute node to compile C code instead of using a login node. Compilation can take from a few seconds to a minute depending on your code's complexity, and doing this on login nodes wastes shared resources that all users need for interactive work [C Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language). 2. **Don't put gcc in job scripts for compilation**: While you should put module loads in your job scripts (not in your `.bashrc`), the compilation step itself should be done on a compute node. You can request an interactive job on a compute node to compile your code, or better yet, use the `test` partition for compilation and testing [Cpp, C++ Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language). **Best practice**: Request an interactive job on a compute node (using `salloc` or `srun`) to compile your C code, then run your actual job script on compute nodes as well. This keeps login nodes fast for everyone and ensures you're using the right resources for compilation.

Question 62: question_62

❓ Question

I want to completely wipe my Julia install off the cluster and start clean. Is there a single command, and does it leave junk behind in my home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Julia/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Julia/Notebook.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gnu-screen, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, there is **no single "uninstall" command** for Julia on the cluster. Julia is installed locally in your `$HOME` directory using the installer script (`curl -fsSL https://install.julialang.org | sh`), and it leaves several artifacts behind. Here's what you need to clean up manually: 1. **The Julia installation directory** — typically `~/.julia` (packages, caches, compiled artifacts) and the `juliaup` directory (if installed via juliaup). 2. **The `julia` executable** — placed in `~/.julia/juliaup/bin/` or similar. 3. **Shell configuration changes** — the installer adds `juliaup` initialization to your `~/.bashrc` (or `~/.zshrc`), which adds Julia's bin directory to your `$PATH`. ### Steps to fully remove Julia: 1. **Remove the Julia home directory:** ```bash rm -rf ~/.julia ``` 2. **Remove the juliaup directory (if present):** ```bash rm -rf ~/.julia/juliaup ``` 3. **Edit your shell config** (`~/.bashrc` or `~/.zshrc`) and remove the lines that source juliaup, typically something like: ```bash # Remove lines containing "juliaup" or "julia" PATH additions ``` 4. **Reload your shell** or start a new session to ensure the changes take effect. There's no official uninstall script provided by FASRC or JuliaLang for this installation method, so manual cleanup is required. The `~/.julia` directory can grow quite large (especially with packages and compiled artifacts), so removing it will free up significant space.
✅ Expected Answer
Run `juliaup self uninstall`. That is the uninstall command, but it does not always clean everything up: afterward, check for the `~/.julia` and `~/.juliaup` directories and delete them manually if they are still there. (This matters because the default Julia install location is $HOME, and the installer also adds Juliaup initialization to your ~/.bashrc to put `julia` on your $PATH.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia

Question 63: question_63

❓ Question

I chmod 777'd a file in my lab's share so a collaborator in a different lab group could grab it, but they still get permission denied when they try to read it. What's going on?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/unix-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The issue is almost certainly **directory permissions on the parent directories**, not the file itself. Here's what's happening: ## The "Directory Traversal" Problem In Unix/Linux, **every directory in the path** to a file must allow the accessing user to traverse it. Even if you `chmod 777` the file itself, your collaborator still needs **execute (x) permission on every parent directory** leading to that file. For example, if the file is at: ``` /n/holylabs/project_name/subdir/filename ``` Your collaborator needs execute permission on: - `/n` - `/holylabs` - `project_name` - `subdir` If any of those directories are set to `drwxr-x---` (no "Other" access), they'll get "Permission denied" before they even reach the file. ## How to Fix It 1. **Check the directory permissions:** ```bash ls -ld /n/holylabs/project_name ls -ld /n/holylabs/project_name/subdir ``` 2. **Grant "Other" execute permission on the parent directories** (not the file): ```bash chmod o+x /n/holylabs/project_name chmod o+x /n/holylabs/project_name/subdir ``` 3. **Keep the file permissions as you set them** (`chmod 777` or more appropriately `chmod 644` for read-only access). ## Important Caveats - **FASSE/L3 shares should never allow "Other" access** — if your data is on those storage tiers, you'll need a different sharing method (like Globus) rather than chmod. - **Consider using Globus** for sharing with external collaborators, as it's designed for this use case and doesn't rely on POSIX permissions [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer). Would you like help checking your specific directory permissions or setting up Globus for sharing?
✅ Expected Answer
Making the file world-readable is not enough on its own. For a user in the 'Other' category to reach a file, they must first be able to traverse into the top-level share/directory that contains it. If the top level of the lab directory or share is set drwxr-x---, drwxrwx---, or drwxr-s--- then Other has no access to the share at all, even if a file inside it is set -rwxrwxrwx. Other must first be able to 'see' into the directory/share; otherwise it has no access. (FASSE/L3 shares should never allow Other access.) To share with someone outside the group, the enclosing directory must permit Other traversal - e.g. the 'Everyone' directory that lab shares provide for exactly this purpose - or the person can be added to your lab Group by your PI.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Question 64: question_64

❓ Question

My labmate keeps his personal scratch folder under Users/ in our old lab share, but our newly created share and netscratch have no Users folder. Where am I supposed to put my own personal directory now?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/parallel-matlab-pct-dcs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vdi-apps, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
In the new FASRC storage structure, the old `Users/` folder has been replaced by two subdirectories inside your lab share: * **`Lab/`** — This is the primary working directory for your lab. Individual users create their personal directories here (e.g., `/n/holylabs/<PI_LAB>/Lab/your_username`). This directory is shared via Globus and is visible to the whole lab. * **`Everyone/`** — This directory is visible to anyone on the HPC cluster and is intended for broader collaboration with other labs. Data here is owned by the lab that hosts it, but it is not available via Globus. So instead of putting your personal files under `Users/your_username`, you should create your personal directory under **`Lab/your_username`** within your lab's share. For example: ```bash mkdir -p /n/holylabs/<PI_LAB>/Lab/your_username ``` This is the recommended location for individual user data, as it keeps things organized within the lab's working directory and maintains proper group access for collaboration. See the [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/) documentation for more details.
✅ Expected Answer
The Users directory only exists on shares created prior to October 2024. On new shares and on /n/netscratch there is no Users directory - FASRC says to use the Lab directory to create personal folders instead. The Lab directory is set drwxrws--- so that every member of the lab group has read/write/execute access to share data amongst the group, and it is the preferred place both for personal folders on new shares and for sharing data with your lab group. On the older shares, the sub-directories in the Users directory are owned by individuals and by default only allow execute access to the group.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Question 65: question_65

❓ Question

I finished the cluster account request form and it never asked me to choose a password. How do I actually get a password to log in?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cant-login-cluster-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/reset-password, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/get-a-fasse-account-and-project-group

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC accounts don't use a traditional password for login — they use **Harvard Key** (your Harvard authentication). Here's what you need to know: **Logging in:** - For most services (CLI, Portal, VDI, OOD), you log in with your FASRC **username** and **password** [User Quick Start Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide/) - Your username was selected at signup, and you set your password separately **Setting your FASRC password:** 1. Go to [https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/p3/pwreset/](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/p3/pwreset/) 2. Enter your email address (the one you signed up with) and click Submit 3. You'll receive an email with a one-time link 4. Click the link and enter a new password **Password requirements:** - At least 12 characters - Both uppercase and lowercase letters - Both a number and a special character (!@#$%^&*) - Must not contain a portion of your name [Reset Password](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/reset-password) If you're having trouble logging in, check the [Login Issues guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cant-login-cluster-access) for common problems.
✅ Expected Answer
Passwords are not collected by the signup application. Your account is created with a random password, and upon completion of the request you use the password reset tool to set your initial password. You will receive an email once the account has been created with links and instructions to do so.

Question 66: question_66

❓ Question

I log into the FASRC web portal in my browser, it accepts my credentials, and then it just spins forever and never shows the menus. Restarting the browser doesn't help. What can I do myself before emailing support?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/virtual-desktop

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, I don't have a specific troubleshooting guide for the FASRC web portal (Open OnDemand) hanging after login. However, here are the standard browser-side steps you can try before emailing support: 1. **Clear your browser cache and cookies** for the portal site (`portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu`). Stale or corrupted session data is a common cause of infinite spinners. 2. **Try a different browser** (e.g., Chrome, Firefox, Edge) or an **incognito/private window** to rule out extensions or cached data interfering. 3. **Check your VPN connection** — if you're accessing the portal from outside the Harvard network, a stable VPN connection is often required for many FASRC services [VPN Setup](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vpn-setup/). 4. **Check for known outages** — FASRC may have a status page or announcements about portal maintenance. If none of these resolve the issue, you'll want to email FASRC support with details about your browser, whether you're on/off campus, and any error messages you see in the browser's developer console (F12 → Console tab).
✅ Expected Answer
If the browser hangs for a long time after authenticating without displaying the Open OnDemand dashboard, restart your Per-User NGINX (PUN) process by visiting: - Cannon: https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/nginx/stop?redir=/pun/sys/dashboard/ - FASSE: https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/nginx/stop?redir=/pun/sys/dashboard/ Also note that the very first login can legitimately take some time while OOD sets up files and cache in your home directory; that Avast security software can cause 'no internet', 'read error', and 'the requested URL could not be retrieved' messages (disable Web Shield); and that Chrome or Firefox are recommended over Safari and Internet Explorer.

Question 67: question_67

❓ Question

My R job asks Slurm for 8 cores, but when it runs my parallel code spawns dozens of workers and the node grinds to a halt. How am I supposed to determine the worker count inside the script?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/parallel-matlab-pct-dcs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/heterogeneous_jobs.html, slurm.schedmd.com/heterogeneous_jobs.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The key is to read the number of cores Slurm allocated for your job and pass that value to your parallel backend. Slurm exposes this via the **`SLURM_NTASKS`** (or **`SLURM_NPROCS`**) environment variable, which is set automatically in your job script. ### How to do it in R 1. **Read the variable in R:** ```R n_cores <- as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_NTASKS")) ``` 2. **Pass it to your parallel backend.** For example, with `parallel` or `future`: ```R library(parallel) cl <- makeCluster(n_cores) ``` Or with `future`: ```R library(future) plan(multisession, workers = n_cores) ``` ### Why this works - When you submit with `#SBATCH --ntasks=8`, Slurm sets `SLURM_NTASKS=8` inside the job environment. - Your R script can read it via `Sys.getenv()`, so the worker count stays in sync with your job allocation. - This prevents your code from spawning more workers than cores, which is what causes the node to "grind to a halt." ### Quick check You can verify the variable is set by adding this to your job script before R runs: ```bash echo "SLURM_NTASKS is $SLURM_NTASKS" ``` If you're using a specific parallel package (e.g., `doParallel`, `future`, `BiocParallel`), let me know and I can give you the exact syntax for that package.
✅ Expected Answer
Do not use parallel::detectCores() to determine the number of CPUs/processes to use in your job - it detects the number of physical CPU cores on the compute node, not the number allocated to your job, which is why you end up oversubscribed. Use parallelly::availableCores() instead, which returns the number of CPUs allocated to your Slurm job.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel

Question 68: question_68

❓ Question

I had openmpi loaded, then loaded mpich later in the same session, and now openmpi is gone from my module list. Did I corrupt my environment, and can I keep both around?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/Python/mpi4py/Example2/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/parigp, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you haven't corrupted your environment. This is expected behavior with FASRC's module system. **Why OpenMPI disappeared:** MPI implementations (OpenMPI and MPICH) are mutually exclusive in the module system. When you load a second MPI module, it replaces the first one rather than stacking them. This is by design because: 1. Both provide the same MPI interface (same commands like `mpicc`, `mpirun`, etc.) 2. Having both loaded simultaneously would cause path conflicts and linking errors 3. Your compiled applications need to link against one specific MPI library **Can you keep both around?** Not simultaneously in the same shell session. However, you can: 1. **Switch back and forth** as needed: ```bash module load openmpi/5.0.2-fasrc01 # or your version # use OpenMPI module unload openmpi/5.0.2-fasrc01 module load mpich/4.2.0-fasrc01 # or your version # use MPICH ``` 2. **Use different sessions** for different MPI implementations 3. **For Python/mpi4py**, you can install `mpi4py` with a specific MPI flavor in a conda environment, which isolates the MPI dependency from your shell modules **Recommendation:** Choose the MPI implementation your application requires and stick with it for that session. If you need to switch, just unload the current MPI module before loading the other. For more details, see the [MPI documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface).
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is corrupted - this is intentional. The modules are set up so that you can only have one MPI module loaded at a time; if you try loading a second one it will automatically unload the first, which is done to avoid dependency collisions. So you cannot keep both loaded: pick one implementation (OpenMPI or MPICH, each built with either the GNU or the Intel compiler suite) and load it with its matching compiler module, e.g. module load gcc/13.2.0-fasrc01 openmpi/5.0.2-fasrc01. Note also that there is no default MPI library in your environment when you log in - you must load one.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Question 69: question_69

❓ Question

I need mpi4py in a conda environment, but it has to be built against the same OpenMPI my Fortran code uses. I just ran mamba create with mpi4py in the package list - what MPI did that actually give me, and how do I get OpenMPI instead?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vdi-apps, slurm.schedmd.com/mpi_guide.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Performance/TAU/Example1/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ood-remote-desktop-how-to-open-software, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/PyTorch/setup_environment.sh, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you ran `mamba create ... mpi4py`, **conda installed `mpi4py` bundled with MPICH** by default — not OpenMPI [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface/). ### How to get `mpi4py` built against OpenMPI instead If your Fortran code uses a specific OpenMPI version, you need to install `mpi4py` via `pip` (not `mamba`) so it links against the OpenMPI module you load: 1. **Load the compiler and OpenMPI modules** you want to match: ```bash module load gcc/12.2.0-fasrc01 module load openmpi/4.1.5-fasrc03 # pick the version your Fortran code uses ``` 2. **Load a Python module** (e.g.): ```bash module load python/3.10.12-fasrc01 ``` 3. **Create a conda environment *without* `mpi4py`**: ```bash mamba create -n python3_env2 python numpy pip wheel source activate python3_env2 ``` 4. **Install `mpi4py` via `pip`** (it will pick up the OpenMPI from your loaded modules): ```bash pip install mpi4py ``` This approach ensures `mpi4py` is compiled against the same OpenMPI implementation your Fortran code uses [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface/). For reference examples, see the FASRC User Codes repo: [Parallel_Computing/Python/mpi4py](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/tree/57c501d3a592f81803f053ff49be41be4c3b/Parallel_Computing/Python/mpi4py). Would you like help picking the right OpenMPI version to match your Fortran setup?
✅ Expected Answer
Putting mpi4py in the mamba create package list (e.g. mamba create -n python3_env1 python numpy pip wheel mpi4py) also installs an MPI library required by mpi4py, and by default that is MPICH - not OpenMPI. If your workflow requires a specific flavor and/or version of MPI, install mpi4py with pip into a custom conda environment instead: load the compiler and MPI modules first (module load gcc/12.2.0-fasrc01; module load openmpi/4.1.5-fasrc03), load a Python module (module load python/3.10.12-fasrc01), create the environment WITHOUT mpi4py (mamba create -n python3_env2 python numpy pip wheel), activate it (source activate python3_env2), then run pip install mpi4py so it builds against the loaded OpenMPI.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Question 70: question_70

❓ Question

I used the 'copy mount path to clipboard' button in Starfish and got /n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab, but when I cd to it on a login node I get 'No such file or directory'. What's the correct path?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The path `/n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab` is the **internal VAST storage path** used by the backend filesystem. On login nodes and compute nodes, this path is **not directly accessible**. Instead, you should use the **mounted path**: **`/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab`** This is the standard path for accessing holylabs storage from the command line. The `/n/vast-holylabs/...` path is an internal representation that Starfish may expose, but it doesn't resolve on login or compute nodes. For more details on FASRC storage paths, see [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage).
✅ Expected Answer
The path Starfish copies is not directly usable - you must make two edits: remove 'vast-' and remove '/C'. So /n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab becomes /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab. The letter C indicates the filesystem is on the Cannon cluster; a group share on FASSE would show F instead of C before the group name.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Question 71: question_71

❓ Question

I want to drag a few files out of my home directory into my lab's netscratch folder using a point-and-click interface, but my home directory doesn't show up as an endpoint in Globus. What else can I use?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Your home directory is intentionally **not available in Globus** for security reasons — sharing it could inadvertently expose authentication keys and passwords. See the [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/) documentation. However, you can still move files between your home directory and your lab's netscratch folder using a point-and-click interface by using **Globus Connect Personal** on your local machine: 1. Install [Globus Connect Personal](https://www.globus.org/globus-connect-personal) on your laptop or desktop. 2. Set it up as a **Mapped Collection** on your local machine, pointing it to your home directory (e.g., `/home/your_username`). 3. In the Globus web interface, you'll now see both your local Mapped Collection (home directory) and the Harvard FAS RC endpoint (which includes your lab's netscratch folder under `Lab/`). 4. You can then drag and drop files between them using the Globus File Manager. This approach lets you use Globus's point-and-click interface without exposing your home directory directly on the FASRC Globus endpoint.
✅ Expected Answer
Globus does not have access to home directories, which is why yours does not appear. For transfers to or from your home directory, use an Open OnDemand Remote Desktop session instead: go to the OOD dashboard (Cannon: rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu, FASSE: fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu), start a Remote Desktop session, open a File Manager by clicking the 'Home' folder on the desktop (or Applications -> File Manager), open a second File Manager window, navigate one to the source (e.g. /n/home01/jharvard/) and the other to the destination (e.g. /n/netscratch/jharvard_lab/Lab/), then right-click the source file -> Copy and right-click the destination window -> Paste (drag-and-drop works too, but behaves like cut-and-paste). Command-line tools (cp, mv, rsync, fpsync) are the alternative; Globus is only recommended for transfers between different filesystems, not within one.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Question 72: question_72

❓ Question

My collaborator is at another university and doesn't have a HarvardKey. Can she still get an account on the cluster, and is there anything I have to keep doing so her access doesn't get shut off?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes, your collaborator can get an account even without a HarvardKey. According to FASRC's account qualifications documentation: - **If she is a faculty member at another university** collaborating with a Harvard PI, she should choose **EXTERNAL** when requesting an account. - **If she is a student or researcher at another university or company** collaborating with a Harvard PI, she should also choose **EXTERNAL** [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/). ### What you need to keep doing As the PI or sponsor, you are responsible for: 1. **Authorizing her access** — Her account must be authorized by you (the Harvard faculty/PI) and approved by FASRC staff [Acceptable Use Policy](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use/). 2. **Monitoring usage** — She agrees to be held accountable by Harvard's electronic access and information security policies, and you should ensure her usage aligns with the research goals of your project [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/). 3. **Keeping her subscribed to notifications** — All active FAS RC account holders are subscribed to the notifications mailing list, which is a requirement for all users [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/). If her affiliation with your lab/project ends, you should notify FASRC so her access can be appropriately managed. For more details, see the [Onboarding Policies and Procedures](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding/).
✅ Expected Answer
Yes. A collaborator without a HarvardKey can apply for an external FASRC account through the account request portal (https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/request/account). External accounts have to be re-enabled every 90 days: the PI must request an extension every 90 days or the account will be suspended. Faculty can sponsor FASRC accounts for anyone working in their lab, including external collaborators.

Question 73: question_73

❓ Question

I'm a new PI with a HarvardKey but the system won't let me approve my grad student's FASRC account request. What do I need to do?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here's what you need to know: **You need to be listed as a PI sponsor in the FASRC system.** Simply having a HarvardKey doesn't automatically give you approval authority. Here's what to do: 1. **Check your PI status in the FASRC system** — You need to be recognized as a PI sponsor. If you're a new PI, your affiliation may not yet be set up in the FASRC portal. 2. **Contact FASRC directly** — Since you're a new PI and the system won't let you approve the request, you'll need to reach out to FASRC support to: - Verify your PI status is properly configured - Have them add you as an approver for your lab group - Or have them manually approve the pending request 3. **Alternative: Have another approved PI sponsor approve it** — If there's already a PI in your group who has approval authority in the system, they can approve the student's request in the meantime. **Important notes from the documentation:** - FASRC accounts are separate from HarvardKey — you need to use the [FAS RC & Informatics Portal](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/request/account/new) for account management [Cluster Account Signup](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup/) - You can only have **one** RC account per person [Cluster Account Signup](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup/) - If you need to add cluster access or change lab groups later, submit a help ticket rather than signing up for a second account [Request an FAS Research Computing Account](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account/) I'd recommend contacting FASRC support directly to get your PI status set up properly so you can approve future requests yourself.
✅ Expected Answer
Complete the FAS Onboard tool for approvers at https://onboard.rc.fas.harvard.edu/onboard/. The onboarding checklist states that if you have a HarvardKey but are denied access to approve new accounts, you should visit and complete the FAS Onboard tool for approvers.

We're wrapping up a project and want to push the raw data off to tape. Before we start, what constraints are there on how the data has to be laid out, and what does it cost per year?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here are the details for tape storage: ## Tape Storage (NESE) Constraints **File layout requirements:** - **10,000 files per folder** maximum - **File sizes between 1 GiB and 100 GiB** each - Data is transferred to tape using **Globus** **Other characteristics:** - No snapshots or disaster recovery - No performance (data is offline/inactive) - Managed externally by Northeast Storage Exchange (NESE) ## Cost **$15/year per TB** This is the most cost-effective storage option for inactive research data after project completion or for data retention purposes. ## Next Steps Before transferring data to tape, you'll need to: 1. Ensure your data meets the file size and folder constraints above 2. Review the [Globus transfer documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/) for instructions on moving data to tape 3. Check your [Coldfront allocations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/coldfront-allocation-management/) to ensure you have the appropriate billing code for tape storage Would you like help with any specific part of the tape transfer process?
✅ Expected Answer
Tape (NESE) storage is sold in 20TB increments, allows ten thousand files per folder, and requires file sizes between 1GiB and 100GiB. It costs $15/yr per TB, is externally managed by the Northeast Storage Exchange (NESE), and data is transferred to tape using Globus. It is intended for long-term storage of inactive research data after project completion or for data retention purposes.

Question 75: question_75

❓ Question

I put a dataset in the Everyone folder so another lab could grab it, but it never shows up in Globus. Is Globus broken?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Globus isn't broken—files placed in the **Everyone** folder are simply **not synced to Globus**. According to FASRC documentation, the Everyone directory is "visible to any one on the HPC cluster and is intended for collaboration with other labs on the cluster," but it is **"not available on Globus and is intended only for internal sharing"** [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs) – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/). Globus only exposes the **Lab** folder by default [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs) – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/). If you want another lab to access your data via Globus, you'll need to either: 1. **Move or copy** the dataset into your Lab folder, or 2. **Share the Globus collection** directly with the other lab's Globus identity (if you've already set up Globus access for your Lab folder). For more on how Globus works with FASRC storage, see [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/).
✅ Expected Answer
No, that is expected behavior. The Everyone subdirectory is visible to anyone on the HPC cluster and is intended only for internal sharing with other labs on the cluster — it is not available on Globus. The Lab subdirectory is the one shared out via Globus, so data that has to move over Globus needs to live under Lab.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center

Is there already a samtools container sitting on the cluster somewhere, or do I have to pull one from Docker Hub myself?

🎯 Retrieval Check

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes—there is already a pre-built **samtools** container available on the cluster via CVMFS. You can use it directly without pulling anything yourself: ```bash singularity exec /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/s/a/samtools:1.13--h8c37831_0 samtools --help ``` This image is hosted on CVMFS at `/cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/s/a/samtools:1.13--h8c37831_0` and is part of the Galaxy Project’s Singularity container library [Singularity – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster/). If you need a different version or want to build your own, you can also pull from Docker Hub and convert it to a Singularity image, but for most use cases the pre-installed version should suffice.
✅ Expected Answer
There is one already on the cluster. Cluster nodes automount a CernVM-File System at /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/, which provides Singularity images for the BioContainers project (auto-generated from Bioconda packages). Images follow the layout /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/FIRST_LETTER/SECOND_LETTER/PACKAGE_NAME:VERSION--CONDA_BUILD, for example: `singularity exec /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/s/a/samtools:1.13--h8c37831_0 samtools --help`. Expect a 10-30 second delay the first time the path is accessed on a compute node that has not mounted/cached it.
